Description
  Text
  Tipranavir is a non-peptidic protease inhibitor (PI) indicated for use in combination with ritonavir and other antiretroviral medications to treat HIV-1 infections in adult and pediatric patients weighing at least 36 kg who are treatment-experienced and resistant to more than one PI. Tipranavir MUST be "boosted" with ritonavir; failure to do so will result in tipranavir plasma concentrations that are insufficient to achieve the desired antiviral effect. The non-peptidic structure of tipranavir theoretically allows for greater flexibility in conforming to HIV protease variants that are resistant to other PIs, and may also decrease the rate of developing resistance. Despite this advantage, guidelines recommend against the use of tipranavir as initial therapy due to its inferior virologic efficacy, higher rate of adverse events, and higher ritonavir dose (200 mg twice daily) required for boosting as compared to other ritonavir-boosted PIs. Risks associated with treatment include hepatic impairment and toxicity, intracranial hemorrhage, and drug interactions due to its effect as an inhibitor of cytochrome P450 3A (CYP3A). Liver function tests are recommended before and during treatment, especially in patients with hepatitis B or C coinfection. Additionally, caution is advised in patients at risk of increased bleeding, those with underlying hepatic impairment, and individuals with a sulfonamide allergy as tipranavir contains a sulfonamide moiety. Tipranavir was approved by the FDA in 2005.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Tipranavir is a non-peptidic protease inhibitor (PI). HIV protease is an enzyme crucial for the maturation of infectious viral particles. This enzyme cleaves large viral Gag and Gag-Pol polyproteins into smaller, functional proteins during the final stages of the HIV life cycle. By inhibiting this process, PIs like tipranavir cause the release of structurally unorganized and noninfectious viral particles. Because HIV PIs inhibit the HIV replication cycle after translation and before assembly, they are active in acutely and chronically infected cells. Compared with peptidic PIs, the non-peptidic structure of tipranavir may allow for greater flexibility in conforming to the active site of the protease enzyme, thereby resulting in activity against PI-resistant HIV and a decreased rate of resistance development.
 
Pharmacodynamics
Tipranavir is effective against HIV-1 isolates with 50% effective concentrations (EC50) ranging between 0.03 to 0.07 microM. However, it shows reduced efficacy against HIV-2 isolates, with EC50 values ranging from 0.233 to 0.522 microM.
 
Resistance
Resistance to tipranavir develops slowly and involves several specific protease gene mutations. In cell cultures, HIV-1 isolates containing the following 10 protease substitutions showed an 87-fold reduced susceptibility to tipranavir: L33F, I84V, K45I, I13V, V32I, V82L, M36I, A71V, L10F, and I54V/T. Six of these mutations (I13V, V32I, L33F, K45I, V82L, I84V) alone conferred more than 10-fold reduced susceptibility. In clinical trials, the most common amino acid substitutions that developed in adults who experienced virologic failures while receiving tipranavir boosted with ritonavir included L33V/I/F, V82T, and I84V. Other, less frequent resistance-associated substitutions included L10V/I/S, I13V, E35D/G/N, I47V, I54A/M/V, K55R, V82L, and L89V/M. Pediatric subjects showed similar resistance patterns. Resistance typically emerged after an average of 38 weeks, with a median 14-fold decrease in tipranavir susceptibility. Cross-resistance among PIs is observed; however, tipranavir maintains less than 4-fold decreased susceptibility against most PI-resistant HIV-1 isolates. Tipranavir-resistant viruses that emerged in vitro had decreased susceptibility to amprenavir, atazanavir, indinavir, lopinavir, nelfinavir, and ritonavir, but remained sensitive to saquinavir.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Tipranavir oral absorption is limited. Administration of tipranavir with ritonavir capsules and a meal (500 to 682 Kcal, 25% fat) had no clinically significant effect on tipranavir maximum plasma concentration (Cmax), trough concentrations (Cmin), or systemic exposure (AUC) when compared to fasted conditions. The effect of food on tipranavir, when administered with ritonavir tablets, has not been evaluated. Steady-state concentrations are attained after 7 to 10 days of twice-daily dosing in most patients. Trough concentrations at steady-state are approximately 70% lower than those on Day 1, presumably due to intestinal P-gp induction.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Patients with mild hepatic insufficiency (Child-Pugh A) were not found to have clinically significant changes in tipranavir pharmacokinetic parameters versus normal controls, and no dosage adjustment is recommended for mild hepatic disease. Tipranavir use is contraindicated in patients with moderate or severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B or C).
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Tipranavir pharmacokinetics have not been studied in patients with renal impairment, but due to negligible renal clearance, renal insufficiency is expected to have no effect on total body clearance of tipranavir.
    Name
    Pediatrics
    Description
    Based on activity and safety data in pediatric subjects, exposure differences between pediatric subjects and adults are not considered to be clinically significant.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    Pharmacokinetic parameters of tipranavir in elderly patients (older than 65 years) do not significantly differ compared to those in younger adult patients.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    Steady-state tipranavir trough concentrations are generally higher in females (median, 43.9 microM) than in males (median, 31.1 microM); however the difference does not warrant a dose adjustment.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    Steady-state tipranavir trough concentrations are more varied in Caucasian males, when compared with Black males; however, the median concentration and the range making up the majority of the data are comparable between races.
    Name
    Other
    Description
    Pregnancy
There are limited pharmacokinetic data regarding use of tipranavir in pregnant women. The drug has moderate placental transfer to the fetus.
  Text
  Tipranavir is administered orally, and each dose is given (boosted) with 200 mg of ritonavir to achieve effective plasma concentrations. Once in systemic circulation, tipranavir is extensively bound (more than 99.9%) to plasma proteins, specifically albumin and alpha1-acid glycoprotein; the unbound fraction appears to be independent of total drug concentration. Tipranavir undergoes hepatic metabolism, predominately by CYP3A4. Coadministration of ritonavir inhibits the CYP3A4 metabolism and intestinal P-glycoprotein (P-gp) efflux of tipranavir, thereby increasing steady-state trough concentrations of tipranavir by 29-fold. In the presence of ritonavir, unchanged tipranavir accounts for at least 98% of the dose circulating at 3, 8, and 12 hours post-dose. A few metabolites have been identified but only at trace levels (0.2% or less) in plasma. Approximately 80% of a tipranavir dose, at steady state, is excreted in the feces; renal excretion is minimal, with approximately 4% recovered in the urine. The effective mean elimination half-life of tipranavir, when administered with ritonavir, is approximately 6 hours at steady state.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP2D6, CYP3A4, CYP3A5, P-gp
Tipranavir is a P-gp substrate, a weak P-gp inhibitor, and appears to be a potent P-gp inducer. Administration with ritonavir appears to result in a net P-gp induction at steady state; even though, when given alone, ritonavir is a P-gp inhibitor. Tipranavir is predominantly metabolized by CYP3A4, and when administered with ritonavir, a CYP3A4 inhibitor, the metabolism is minimal. When administered with ritonavir, tipranavir has many effects on the CYP isoenzyme system. It is a potent inhibitor of hepatic and intestinal CYP3A4, hepatic and intestinal CYP3A5, and CYP2D6. Marked induction of CYP2C19 and moderate induction of CYP1A2 is also observed. Tipranavir has no net effect on CYP2C9.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
 
NOTE: Tipranavir is indicated for use in conjunction with ritonavir to assure appropriate tipranavir concentrations. DO NOT use unless coadministered with ritonavir. Failure to administer tipranavir with ritonavir may result in a loss of virologic efficacy.
NOTE: Interruptions of therapy may be necessary for specific situations.
If an unanticipated short-term treatment interruption (i.e., less than 1 to 2 days) is necessary, it is generally recommended that all antiretroviral agents be discontinued simultaneously, especially if the interruption occurs in a pregnant patient or is because of serious toxicity.
If a short-term treatment interruption is anticipated, as in the case of elective surgery, consider the pharmacokinetic properties and food requirements of the specific antiretroviral medications. Stopping a treatment regimen containing medications with differing half-lives may result in functional monotherapy of the antiretroviral with the longest half-life and may increase the risk for resistant mutations.
Health care providers are advised to restart a complete and effective antiretroviral regimen as soon as possible after a short-term interruption of therapy.
Planned long-term treatment interruptions are not recommended due to the potential for HIV disease progression (i.e., declining CD4 counts, viral rebound, acute viral syndrome), development of minor HIV-associated manifestations or serious non-AIDS complications, development of drug resistance, increased risk of HIV transmission, and increased risk for opportunistic infections.
If therapy must be discontinued, counsel the patient on the potential risks and closely monitor for any clinical or laboratory abnormalities.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Administration Details
      Name
      Oral Solid Formulations
      Description
      Swallow capsules whole; do not open or chew.
Assess children for their ability to swallow capsules before prescribing tipranavir.
    Name
    Oral Administration
    Description
    Ensure dose is administered with a meal. Failure to administer with food may result in loss of virologic efficacy.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Mild hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh A): No dosage adjustment is required in patients with mild hepatic impairment; however, use caution and monitor closely as increased concentrations may be seen.
Moderate and severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh B or C): Use is contraindicated.
  Renal Impairment
  Specific guidelines for dosage adjustments in renal impairment are not available; it appears that no dosage adjustments are needed.
